9 
Q. 
By whom are you employed and in what capacity? 
10 
A. 
I am employed by National Grid USA Service Company, Inc. (“Service Company”), a 
11 
subsidiary of National Grid USA (“National Grid”), as New York Gas Chief Operating 
12 
Officer.  In this role, I oversee approximately 3,800 employees and more than $17.5 billion 
13 
of gas infrastructure assets serving customers across New York State. 
14 
 
15 
National Grid owns and operates three gas distribution companies in New York that 
16 
provide retail gas service to more than 2.5 million customers in three service territories: 
17 
Niagara Mohawk Power Corporation d/b/a National Grid (“Niagara Mohawk”) serves 
18 
areas of eastern and central New York; The Brooklyn Union Gas Company d/b/a National 
19 
Grid NY (“KEDNY”) serves Brooklyn, Staten Island, and parts of Queens in New York 
20 
City; and KeySpan Gas East Corporation d/b/a National Grid (“KEDLI” or the 
21 
“Company”) (KEDNY and KEDLI are collectively referred to as the “Companies”) serves 
22 
customers on Long Island and the Rockaway Peninsula in Queens.  I am responsible for 
23

7 
II. 
Purpose of Testimony 
8 
Q. 
What is the purpose of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel’s testimony? 
9 
A. 
The purpose of the Panel’s testimony is to provide a forecast of the Company’s capital 
10 
investments for the twelve-month period ending March 31, 2025 (“Rate Year”) and the 
11 
three subsequent twelve-month periods ending March 31, 2026 (“Data Year 1”), March 31, 
12 
2027 (“Data Year 2”), and March 31, 2028 (“Data Year 3”) (Data Year 1, Data Year 2, and 
13 
Data Year 3 are collectively referred to as the “Data Years”).  The Panel also presents a 
14 
forecast for the 15-month period (“Link Period”) between December 31, 2022, which is 
15 
the end of the Company’s Historic Test Year, and the beginning of the proposed Rate Year 
16 
on April 1, 2024. 
17 
 
18 
The Panel discusses direct capital expenditures in projects and programs that will: (i) 
19 
increase the safety and reliability of the Company’s gas network; (ii) modernize the 
20 
Company’s gas transmission and distribution infrastructure; (iii) facilitate emissions 
21 
reductions; and (iv) further Commission and state policy goals, including the goals of the 
22 
Climate Leadership and Community Protection Act (“CLCPA”).  The Panel will also 
23 


--- Page 9 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 7 of 105 
discuss the Company’s practices and policies for maximizing the efficiency of its capital 
1 
delivery program from planning and budgeting through the completion of construction, as 
2 
well as efforts to identify alternatives to new investments in gas infrastructure, such as non-
3 
pipes alternatives (“NPAs”). 
4 
 
5 
 
The Panel’s testimony provides an overview of the notable projects in the Company’s 
6 
capital plan, including retirement of leak-prone pipe (“LPP”), projects to reduce leaks, and 
7 
new safety programs to identify and address system risks.  The Panel’s testimony also 
8 
presents an overview of the Company’s pipeline integrity and reliability programs that will 
9 
improve the safety and reliability of the Company’s gas system, and addresses recently 
10 
enacted pipeline safety regulations administered by the U.S. Department of Transportation 
11 
(“DOT”), Pipeline and Hazardous Materials Safety Administration (“PHMSA”). 
12 
 
13 
The Panel also sets forth the Company’s proposals directed at: (i) facilitating the 
14 
displacement of fossil fuel with low-carbon energy through a hydrogen blending pilot 
15 
project, and (ii) enabling renewable natural gas (“RNG”) interconnections to reduce 
16 
emissions while continuing to meet customer needs.  The Panel also discusses the 
17 
Company’s Utility Thermal Energy Network and Jobs Act pilot proposal, which is being 
18 
considered separately by the Commission in Case 22-M-0429, and requests approval of the 
19 
FTEs needed to implement those pilots. 
20 
 
21 
 
The Panel also presents the Company’s indirect capital investments including inventory 
22 
management/warehouse management, Information Technology, fleet, and facilities 
23 


--- Page 10 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 8 of 105 
investments.  The Company is proposing investments in facilities and fleet during the Rate 
1 
Year and Data Years that are necessary to upgrade aging facilities, including safety and 
2 
energy efficiency enhancements, and appropriately size the number of vehicles, including 
3 
a program to electrify a portion of the Company’s fleet. 
4 
 
5 
Q. 
Does the Panel’s testimony also address the Company’s operations and maintenance 
6 
(“O&M”) programs? 
7 
A. 
Yes.  In addition to capital investments, the Panel describes incremental labor (full-time 
8 
equivalent positions or “FTEs”) and non-labor O&M expenses that the Company proposes 
9 
in the Rate Year and Data Years, the costs of which are not fully reflected in the twelve-
10 
month period beginning January 1, 2022 and ending December 31, 2022 (“Historic Test 
11 
Year”).  These expenses represent known and measurable changes from Historic Test Year 
12 
expenses that are necessary to: (i) address new and emerging safety regulations; (ii) 
13 
enhance the service provided to our customers; and (iii) support the Company’s capital 
14 
investments.  The Panel will also discuss the Company’s staffing plan for the proposed 
15 
new FTEs. 
16 
 
17

Q. 
How is the Panel’s testimony organized? 
1 
A. 
The testimony is organized into the following sections: 
2 
Sections I and II are introductory sections outlining the Panel’s testimony. 
3 
Section III provides an overview of the Company’s capital investment and O&M 
4 
program priorities and objectives, strategy to address increasing costs, and significant 
5 
capital programs, such as the retirement of leak-prone mains and services, and other 
6 
key capital investments in gas pipeline safety and reliability.  This section also 
7 
describes the Company’s efforts to support the goals and priorities of the CLCPA in its 
8 
investment planning and operations. 
9 
Section IV provides details on the Company’s proposed direct capital investment 
10 
program for the Rate Year and Data Years, including the Company’s spending 
11 
rationales, categories of capital investment, and specific work activities within each 
12 
category. 
13 
Section V describes the Company’s indirect capital investment plan, which includes 
14 
facilities, fleet, and supply chain investments. 
15 
Section VI describes the Company’s O&M programs, including those necessary to 
16 
carry out the Company’s proposed capital programs and those targeted at current and 
17 
emerging safety regulations and implementation of gas pipeline-safety related best 
18 
practices.  With respect to gas pipeline safety, some of the O&M expenditures 
19 
presented in this Panel’s testimony and set forth in Exhibit __ (GIOP-6) and Exhibit __ 
20 
(GIOP-7) are presented in more detail in the Companies’ Gas Safety Panel testimony. 
21 
 
22

III. 
Capital and O&M Plan Objectives and Priorities 
1 
A. Plan Objectives 
2 
Q. 
Please describe the overall objective of the Company’s infrastructure and operations 
3 
programs. 
4 
A. 
The Company’s gas infrastructure and operations programs are designed to provide safe 
5 
and reliable gas delivery service to customers at reasonable costs.  Over the last several 
6 
years, the Company increased investments to modernize and enhance the resiliency of its 
7 
gas assets, some of which are over 50 years old. 
8 
 
9 
Capital investment over the next several years is required to ensure that the gas system 
10 
continues to operate safely and further modernize the Company’s facilities in a manner that 
11 
achieves meaningful methane emissions reductions.  The majority of the proposed capital 
12 
investments and O&M costs are required to satisfy state and federal regulatory 
13 
requirements and goals, including retirement of LPP, gas pipeline safety enhancements, 
14 
and deployment of NPAs.  Figure 1 below presents the percentage of capital investments 
15 
for the eight major capital investment categories during the Rate Year. 
16 
 
17 
 
18 
 
19 
 
20 
 
21 
 
22 
 
23 


--- Page 14 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 12 of 105 
Figure 1:  Rate Year Capital Investment by Major Category 
1 
 
2 
Although these investments are needed to meet various requirements and objectives, in 
3 
developing its capital and O&M plans, the Company undertook all reasonable efforts to 
4 
manage costs and minimize impacts on customer rates, where possible. 
5 
 
6 
Q. 
What are the Company’s projected direct and indirect capital forecasts? 
7 
A. 
A summary of the Company’s total capital plan forecast for the Rate Year and Data Years 
8 
is provided in Table 1.

Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 13 of 105 
Q. 
Please discuss the need for the levels of capital investments set forth in the rate filing. 
1 
A. 
Over the last decade, several developments have required KEDLI and other natural gas 
2 
distribution utilities to increase their annual capital expenditures.  Notably, pipeline safety 
3 
incidents, such as the tragic events in San Bruno, California, Allentown, Pennsylvania, and 
4 
more recent incidents, including East Harlem, New York and Merrimack Valley, 
5 
Massachusetts, have appropriately increased focus on pipeline safety and the need to 
6 
carefully monitor and replace aging or at risk pipeline infrastructure.  Weather events such 
7 
as Superstorm Sandy, Hurricanes Irene and Lee, and the Polar Vortex, and the expectation 
8 
that similar events will continue to occur, require the Company to find ways to protect its 
9 
facilities from severe weather.  Indeed, recent weather events involving extreme cold 
10 
temperatures, including Winter Storm Elliott in December 2022, highlight the need for 
11 
more reinforcement and resiliency projects to ensure the continued provision of safe and 
12 
reliable service to customers. 
13 
 
14 
In response to these events, pipeline safety regulators have adopted new and expanded 
15 
safety requirements.  For example, since the 2019 Rate Case, PHMSA issued new safety 
16 
requirements related to transmission system integrity management and verification.  The 
17 
Commission also issued a Memorandum and Resolution that significantly increased the 
18 
requirements for utility operator qualification or “OQ” programs.  These required 
19 
improvements necessitate additional investments and compliance resources to ensure the 
20 
Company can continue to provide safe and reliable service to customers consistent with 
21 
regulatory obligations. 
22 
 
23 


--- Page 16 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 14 of 105 
In addition to new requirements, the costs of delivering mandated programs have increased 
1 
for a variety of reasons, including inflation, supply chain constraints, contractor prices due 
2 
to OQ requirements, and increased paving requirements.  These new mandates, coupled 
3 
with increasing maintenance and construction costs, are the main drivers of the proposed 
4 
increases to capital investments and O&M programs over the next several years.  The 
5 
factors contributing to the increases and the Company’s plan to mitigate rising costs are 
6 
presented in more detail below. 
7 
 
8 
Q. 
How does the Company support the level of capital investment? 
9 
A. 
As the Company developed plans to modernize its gas assets, it also began to build and 
10 
enhance its operations, engineering, resource management, work management, and quality 
11 
control organizations and capabilities to deliver increasing levels of capital investment.  
12 
The Company will continue to develop these capabilities in the Rate Year and Data Years 
13 
by adding incremental labor resources to execute the capital plan and support the increased 
14 
operations workload (discussed in Section VI).  The capital investment plan includes 
15 
additional field training resources, facilities, and tools to enhance the development of the 
16 
internal work force. 
17 
 
18 
Regarding contractor resources, over the past several years, the Company developed a 
19 
procurement strategy that supports sustainable growth in qualified contractors to meet the 
20 
work plan increases.  To ensure adequate levels of qualified, skilled labor and the 
21 
challenges around developing qualified contractors, the Company has worked to: 
22 


--- Page 17 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 15 of 105 
 Establish longer term contracts (which will be effective during the Rate Year and 
1 
Data Years) to enable contractors to plan and invest in hiring, training, facilities, 
2 
and equipment to meet the Company’s construction needs; 
3 
 Provide greater work plan visibility to contractors on forecast crew requirements 
4 
that will enable them to develop the required capacity; 
5 
 Manage the work plan to limit seasonal variability to support a stable contractor 
6 
workforce and promote worker retention; and 
7 
 Continue partnerships with community colleges, trade schools, and veterans’ 
8 
groups to attract and develop new sources of skilled labor to build the workforce. 
9 
 
10 
Q. 
Does the Company require additional personnel in the Rate Year and Data Years to 
11 
execute its capital and O&M programs? 
12 
A. 
Yes.  The Company forecasts the need for an additional 59 gas FTE positions in the Rate 
13 
Year, increasing to 89 in Data Year 3, to support the proposed capital investments, 
14 
increasing O&M workload due to new and pending regulatory requirements, new programs 
15 
discussed below, and the gas safety programs presented in the Gas Safety Panel testimony.  
16 
These FTEs include positions in advanced leak detection, operator qualifications, learning 
17 
and development, city state construction, and pipeline safety and compliance.  The FTEs 
18 
will be charged to both capital and O&M programs based on the job function and nature 
19 
of the work.  Exhibit __ (GIOP-7) identifies the incremental FTE positions by function and 
20 
the need for each position.  Labor O&M associated with the FTEs is presented in the 
21 
Revenue Requirements Panel’s testimony and exhibits. 
22 
 
23 


--- Page 18 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 16 of 105 
B. Supporting the CLCPA 
1 
Q. 
Please briefly describe the CLCPA and its impact on utility operations and regulatory 
2 
proceedings in New York. 
3 
A. 
Signed into law in 2019, the CLCPA is New York’s landmark climate change legislation 
4 
that mandates a statewide greenhouse gas (“GHG”) emissions reduction of 40 percent by 
5 
2030 and at least 85 percent by 2050 from 1990 emissions levels.  To accomplish these 
6 
emissions reduction targets, the CLCPA promotes the use of renewable energy sources, 
7 
energy efficiency measures, and zero emissions transportation options.  The CLCPA is also 
8 
focused on creating clean energy sector jobs in New York State, improving air quality, and 
9 
mitigating any potential disproportionate impacts of the clean energy transition on 
10 
Disadvantaged Communities.  The CLCPA requires that administrative agencies, such as 
11 
the Commission, consider whether their administrative approvals and decisions comply 
12 
with the CLCPA’s requirements and long-term goals. 
13 
 
14 
 
Although the CLCPA has been widely discussed over the past few years, legislators, 
15 
regulators, and stakeholders are still developing the regulations and implementation plans 
16 
that will achieve the CLCPA’s aggressive, long-term emissions reductions and 
17 
electrification goals.  In its recently issued Final Scoping Plan, the Climate Action Council, 
18 
the body charged with developing the CLCPA implementation framework, highlighted the 
19 
complexities and challenges associated with the State’s clean energy transition, including 
20 
customer affordability and reliability concerns.  Both the Climate Action Council and the 
21 
Commission have reinforced that the safety and reliability of the gas system is imperative 
22 


--- Page 19 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 17 of 105 
during this transition, and continued investment in the gas system is required to ensure the 
1 
safe and reliable operation of the system and to reduce GHG emissions. 
2 
 
3 
Q. 
Please highlight some of the investments and programs that advance CLCPA 
4 
requirements. 
5 
A. 
The Company is committed to initiatives that support a just and affordable clean energy 
6 
transition.  Accordingly, the Company’s entire investment plan was considered in light of 
7 
CLCPA goals and priorities, focusing on projects, programs, and initiatives that will reduce 
8 
system emissions and decarbonize the gas supply, while ensuring the continued provision 
9 
of safe and reliable service.  Some key features of the investment plan that will support 
10 
CLCPA priorities include: 
11 
 Emissions Reductions – In addition to providing safety and reliability benefits, the LPP 
12 
retirement and leak management programs will directly reduce emissions from the 
13 
Company’s gas network.  These programs have reduced direct system emissions by 32 
14 
percent since 2013.  The Company is also proposing new investments and programs, 
15 
like the Advanced Leak Detection and Advanced Communications Infrastructure 
16 
programs (discussed by the Gas Safety Panel), specifically targeting high-emitting gas 
17 
leaks and reducing system emissions. 
18 
 Emissions Reporting – Consistent with the commitments made in the 2019 Rate Case, 
19 
the Company’s capital proposal is supported by a GHG assessment for the proposed 
20 
projects and programs, which will allow the Commission and other stakeholders to 
21 
consider the emission impacts of proposed investments. 
22 


--- Page 20 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 18 of 105 
 Disadvantaged Communities – Large portions of the Company’s service territory 
1 
consist of areas designated as Disadvantaged Communities, and the proposed 
2 
infrastructure investments will upgrade facilities in these neighborhoods.  The 
3 
investments will reduce leaks and local GHG emissions, improve air quality, and 
4 
enhance the safety and reliability of service to customers in those communities who 
5 
rely on gas service to affordably heat their homes.  Where a particular project is located 
6 
in a Disadvantaged Community, that information is noted in the project data sheet in 
7 
Exhibit __ (GIOP-5).  The direct testimonies of the Customer and CLCPA Panels also 
8 
describe the Company’s proposals for affordability, energy efficiency, and customer 
9 
service programs specifically supporting Disadvantaged Communities. 
10 
 Net Zero and Alternative Investments – The Company has incorporated NPAs into its 
11 
capital planning processes and looks for opportunities to avoid installation or 
12 
replacement of traditional gas infrastructure by using alternatives, such as geothermal 
13 
heat pumps or electrified solutions.  In August 2022, the Company submitted filings, 
14 
individually and with the Joint Utilities, in the Commission’s Gas Planning Proceeding 
15 
(Case 20-G-0131) regarding NPA evaluation criteria, incentive mechanisms, and cost 
16 
recovery.  The Company will incorporate any requirements and guidance regarding 
17 
NPAs from that proceeding into its capital planning processes.  The Company will 
18 
continue to look for opportunities to advance NPAs in the Rate Year and Data Years 
19 
and proposes to continue current NPA obligations, such as the annual obligations to 
20 
submit a request for proposal for NPA solutions and to identify five LPP projects that 
21 
could be replaced with NPAs.  The Company also proposes to continue the existing 
22 
electrification referral program for new customers in conjunction with Consolidated 
23

Edison Company of New York, Inc. (“Con Edison”) and PSE&G-Long Island/Long 
1 
Island Power Authority (“PSEG-LI/LIPA”). 
2 
 Future of Heat – Finally, the Company proposes several Future of Heat investments to 
3 
support the energy transition, decarbonize the gas supply, and reduce GHG emissions, 
4 
including projects to facilitate RNG interconnections and to expand the existing green 
5 
hydrogen blending project with the Town of Hempstead.

Q. 
Does the Panel address the GHG emissions impacts of the investments and initiatives 
8 
presented in your testimony? 
9 
A. 
Yes.  Many of the investments and initiatives described by the Panel will reduce GHG 
10 
emissions.  Some of the projects will have direct emissions impacts that can be quantified 
11 
using relatively straightforward methodologies, such as the LPP program.  Conversely, 
12 
some initiatives that directly reduce GHG emissions are less amenable to quantification 
13 
(e.g., the Advanced Leak Detection program). 
14 
 
15 
Other investments and initiatives will have indirect impacts on GHG reductions.  For 
16 
example, investments in telecommunications upgrades or advanced communication 
17 
infrastructure are expected to facilitate more efficient operations and improve the ability to 
18 
detect and respond to leaks; however, these investments will not directly reduce GHG 
19 
emissions in the near term. 
20 
 
21 
Other investments may be needed to ensure that KEDLI can continue to provide safe and 
22 
reliable service and are expected to have little or no impact on GHG emissions (e.g., 
23 


--- Page 22 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 20 of 105 
operator qualification training).  Some of the projects or programs supported by the Panel’s 
1 
testimony may nominally increase emissions on a temporary or sustained basis, but the 
2 
investments are necessary to enable KEDLI to continue to provide safe and reliable gas 
3 
service to customers (e.g., LNG heating equipment). 
4 
 
5 
An assessment of the GHG emissions associated with the specific investment areas 
6 
supported by the Panel is presented in the project data sheets in Exhibit __ (GIOP-5).  The 
7 
testimony of the CLCPA Panel also summarizes the GHG emissions impacts of the 
8 
investments.

C. Cost Pressures and Cost Mitigation 
11 
Q. 
What explains the cost increases reflected in the investment plan? 
12 
A. 
There are several factors that are collectively increasing the cost of the Company’s 
13 
investment plan over the next several years.  These factors include: (i) incremental work 
14 
driven by federal and state regulatory mandates; (ii) the need to upgrade aged infrastructure 
15 
and replace dated critical facilities; (iii) contractor increases related to new work 
16 
requirements and enhanced safety practices; and (iv) inflation and the rising costs of 
17 
materials, in part due to supply chain issues and increasing demands for these resources 
18 
across the industry. 
19 
 
20 
A key driver of the capital plan increases is significantly higher unit costs for main 
21 
installation work across multiple mandated programs, including proactive and reactive 
22 


--- Page 23 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 21 of 105 
main replacement, customer connections, corrosion, and CSC programs.  The unit cost 
1 
increases are explained in more detail, below, and are shown on Exhibit __ (GIOP-2). 
2 
 
3 
Q. 
What factors are contributing to the Company’s unit cost increases? 
4 
A. 
Exhibit __ (GIOP-2) shows the components that have contributed to the main installation 
5 
unit cost increase.  The primary source of increased costs in KEDLI’s New York City 
6 
portion of its service territory is New York City’s restoration and paving requirements.  
7 
Specifically, changes to Highway Rules §§ 2-01 through 2-09, 2-11 through 2-14, and 2-
8 
20 (“Paving Rules”), which require significantly more restoration work, including: 
9 
 Increasing the size of the area that must be re-paved following an excavation.  Under 
10 
the Paving Rules, the final restoration area is based on the largest dimension of the 
11 
excavation.  Under the prior regulations, KEDLI was authorized to pave the area of 
12 
individual bell holes.  The Paving Rules require KEDLI to pave the bell holes in 
13 
addition to the area in between those holes.  In some cases, this requirement doubles 
14 
and triples the area needing to be replaced; 
15 
 Requiring that base material be replaced in kind or better, which means that concrete 
16 
roadways must be replaced with concrete instead of the less expensive binder material 
17 
that was previously used.  In that regard, approximately 65 percent of New York City 
18 
streets are concrete; and 
19 
 Requiring additional saw cutting of roadway excavations instead of more efficient and 
20 
cost-effective jackhammering. 
21 
 
22 


--- Page 24 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 22 of 105 
The operational and financial impacts of the Paving Rules have been fully realized over 
1 
the term of the current rate plan and, therefore, are reflected in the historic average unit 
2 
costs used to develop the forecasts presented in this filing. 
3 
 
4 
 
Other factors contributing to the unit cost increase as shown in Exhibit __ (GIOP-2) 
5 
include: 
6 
 Higher labor costs in recently renegotiated construction contracts; and 
7 
 Additional 
municipal 
rules 
for 
working 
around 
green 
infrastructure 
8 
(bioswales/drainage), including restoration, materials, and pedestrian accessibility 
9 
requirements. 
10 
 
11 
Q. 
What is the Company doing to moderate the size of its investment plan? 
12 
A. 
As set forth in more detail in Section IV, the Company’s proposed capital and O&M 
13 
investment plans are developed through close coordination between the Asset 
14 
Management, Field Operations, and Investment & Resource Management teams.  Major 
15 
programs and investments undergo a robust options analysis, including an analysis of 
16 
potential NPAs, to identify the most cost-effective solution.  Alternatives considered, as 
17 
well as project benefit information for major capital investments, are set forth in the data 
18 
sheets included in Exhibit __ (GIOP-5).  The Companies’ Capital Delivery organization is 
19 
tasked with systematically improving the Company’s ability to develop and execute its 
20 
capital plan more efficiently for complex projects.  The Capital Delivery team continues to 
21 
work on identifying efficiencies and opportunities for better ways of working and 
22 
achieving synergies targeted to reduce costs. 
23 


--- Page 25 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 23 of 105 
Q. 
Is the Company proposing any unit cost productivity incentives to encourage 
1 
attainment of efficiencies? 
2 
A. 
Yes.  The Company is proposing a unit cost productivity incentive for the Proactive Main 
3 
Replacement Program, similar to the incentive approved in the 2016 Rate Case.  Proactive 
4 
LPP replacement is critical to improving the safety and reliability of the Company’s system 
5 
and achieving meaningful emissions reductions.  As discussed below and in the testimony 
6 
of the Gas Safety Panel, the Company is proposing to target a minimum of 456 miles 
7 
between calendar year (“CY”) 2024 and CY 2027 through the program to reduce its LPP 
8 
inventory.  Although LPP replacement work is necessary over the coming years, the costs 
9 
associated with this work continue to increase due to external factors, such as additional 
10 
safety requirements and paving requirements, contractor and material costs, and inflation.  
11 
A unit cost incentive will challenge the Company to complete this important work in a 
12 
more efficient manner, while allowing customers and the Company to share the 
13 
environmental and safety benefits, achieved through completion of this work.  The 
14 
incentive would only apply to work completed through the Proactive Main Replacement 
15 
Program and would not apply to other capital programs or any LPP replacements projects 
16 
replaced by NPAs.  KEDLI’s eligibility for the LPP Productivity Incentive each year would 
17 
be conditioned upon the Company removing the minimum LPP removal target established 
18 
in the rate plan for that year.  Unit costs for purposes of the incentive would be calculated 
19 
by dividing KEDLI’s actual costs for proactive LPP removals by the total number of LPP 
20 
feet removed from service through KEDLI’s proactive main replacement program in the 
21 
relevant year. 
22 
 
23 


--- Page 26 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 24 of 105 
 
KEDLI’s rates reflect the following unit costs (exclusive of associated O&M expense) for 
1 
LPP replacements: FY 2025 – $480/foot, FY 2026 – $493/foot, FY 2027 – $506/foot, and 
2 
FY 2028 – $520/foot.  Under the mechanism, the Company proposes to earn a positive 
3 
revenue adjustment of up to ten basis points if it achieves unit cost savings for LPP 
4 
replacements in a given year as compared to the unit costs reflected in rates.  The 
5 
mechanism calculates the incentive across six tiers of increasingly challenging levels of 
6 
unit cost savings.  The proposed incentive mechanism for the Rate Year and Data Years is 
7 
presented in Table 2 below and Exhibit __ (GIOP-3), page 6 of 10.  The incentive would 
8 
be recovered through the Company’s Gas Safety and Reliability Surcharge (“GSRS”), 
9 
which is discussed in the testimony of the Rate Design Panel.

Q. 
How are the unit costs used for the productivity incentives derived? 
1 
A. 
The unit cost calculations are provided in Exhibit __ (GIOP-3).  The incentive mechanism 
2 
uses the total unit costs for main installations, including the costs of additional safety 
3 
programs that, for accounting purposes, will ultimately be charged to the work orders for 
4 
main replacements.  For purposes of presentation of the capital investment plan in this 
5 
proceeding, Exhibit __ (GIOP-1) presents the costs of these additional programs, such as 
6 
the OQ program, as separate line items for clarity of costs attributed to each program.  For 
7 
the purpose of setting productivity incentives, however, unit costs must include an 
8 
allocation of costs for the additional safety programs to accurately reflect all costs that will 
9 
be charged to the work orders for main and service replacements. 
10 
 
11 
As shown in Exhibit __ (GIOP-3), unit costs for the LPP productivity incentive include the 
12 
costs for the Proactive Main Replacement Program, plus allocated costs for the OQ 
13 
program. 
14 
 
15 
Q. 
Are there potential safety and regulatory changes on the horizon that may further 
16 
increase capital and O&M requirements during the Rate Year and Data Years? 
17 
A. 
Yes.  The Company has not fully included the costs to comply with PHMSA Mega Rule 2 
18 
(RIN 2137-AF39) in its proposed investment plan, which will impact the Company’s 
19 
transmission and distribution operations and was recently “stayed” by PHMSA to allow 
20 
more time for agency guidance and implementation.  The Company has followed the 
21 
PHMSA proceeding and, where possible, worked to identify and incorporate the associated 
22 
changes into its plan.  The Company has included costs associated with transmission 
23

Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel

system and Integrity Management Program (“IMP”) requirements in its capital and 
1 
operating plan (e.g., enhanced risk modeling, repair criteria) as many of these practices 
2 
were already included in the Company’s procedures.  Nevertheless, not all of the 
3 
incremental costs resulting from the new pipeline integrity regulations are reflected due to 
4 
the stay of enforcement and the potential for revised interpretations once the rule becomes 
5 
effective. 
6 
 
7 
Additionally, on March 16, 2023, the Commission commenced a proceeding applicable to 
8 
all gas utilities in Case 23-G-0083 that will review installation of certain tapping tees.  The 
9 
proceeding was opened too recently for the Company to complete an analysis of the

of this filing.

13

15 
changes for future recovery.

The capital plan represents the investments required to provide safe and reliable service to

21 
the following: 
22 
 Proactive Main Replacement (LPP) Program;

Notably, the capital plan includes a suite of gas safety-related capital investments in 
4

5

6

10 
 
11 
i. 
Proactive Main Replacement (LPP) Program 
12 
Q. 
Please describe the inventory of LPP existing on the Company’s system. 
13 
A. 
LPP in KEDLI’s service territory is comprised of twelve-inch and smaller main that is: (i) 
14 
unprotected (i.e., non-cathodically protected) steel/wrought iron pipe (whether bare or 
15 
coated); (ii) cast and wrought iron pipe; (iii) pre-1985 vintage Aldyl-A plastic pipe; and 
16 
(iv) associated non-cathodically protected steel/wrought steel, copper, cast iron, pre-1974 
17 
high-density polyethylene (“HDPE”) and pre-1985 Aldyl-A plastic services.  According to 
18 
PHMSA data, New York has among the highest concentrations of LPP in the country, a 
19 
significant portion of which is located in the Company’s service territory.  As of the end of 
20 
CY 2022, the Company has approximately 3,021 miles of LPP remaining in its inventory. 
21 
 
22 
 
23 


--- Page 30 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 28 of 105 
Q. 
What is the Company’s proposal regarding retirement of LPP? 
1 
A. 
To reduce the risk of leaks and breaks, improve system safety, performance, and reliability, 
2 
as well as meet the Company’s commitment to enhance customer satisfaction and reduce 
3 
methane emissions, the Company has prioritized the retirement of older and higher-risk 
4 
gas infrastructure – specifically, LPP and associated services – that disproportionally 
5 
contribute to leaks on KEDLI’s system. 
6 
 
7 
 
The current plan, approved by the Commission in the 2019 Rate Case, requires retirement 
8 
of at least 477 total miles of LPP during CY 2020 through CY 2023, collectively. 
9 
 
10 
The Company proposes to continue a steady pace of LPP retirement in the Rate Year and 
11 
Data Years.  Specifically, the Company proposes annual minimum mileage targets and a 
12 
cumulative minimum mileage target of 456 miles over the four-year period from CY 2024 
13 
to CY 2027.  The Company believes these targets are reasonable as the program will 
14 
continue the robust retirement of LPP, and the benefits associated with LPP retirement, 
15 
while also being mindful of the associated costs.  The metrics and targets for LPP 
16 
retirement, including minimum mileage targets, are set forth in more detail in the Gas 
17 
Safety Panel testimony.  The proposed LPP retirement rate will position the Company to 
18 
complete full retirement of the remaining LPP inventory by approximately 2045. 
19 
 
20 
Q. 
Does LPP retirement align with CLCPA goals and the Final Scoping Plan? 
21 
A. 
Yes.  Retirement of LPP reduces gas losses and fugitive emissions of methane, which 
22 
further the objectives of the CLCPA.  The Climate Action Council’s Final Scoping Plan 
23 


--- Page 31 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 29 of 105 
highlighted the importance of LPP efforts, noting that such retirements are necessary for 
1 
safety reasons and will produce real emissions reductions.  Indeed, since 2013, KEDLI’s 
2 
system methane emissions have decreased by an estimated 32 percent, due in large part to 
3 
this program.  Table 3 provides a high-level estimate of potential methane emissions 
4 
reductions over the next several years assuming the retirement of LPP pursuant to KEDLI’s 
5 
proposed program. 
6

Q. 
Why is it important from a safety and environmental perspective to continue LPP 
1 
retirement in the Rate Year and Data Years? 
2 
A. 
As the Company has worked over the last several years to retire the lowest performing 
3 
LPP, the Company has observed a reduction in the overall leak rate, as shown in Table 4.  
4 
While leak rates on LPP still outpace the leak rate for overall distribution piping, the leak 
5 
rates have not continued to rise over the past several years.  The current leak rate for all 
6 
distribution piping is 0.05 leaks per mile, excluding damages from excavation.  The 2022 
7 
leak rate for LPP was 0.14 leaks per mile. 
8 
 
9 
Because the leak rates have decreased over the past several years, KEDLI’s proposal is a 
10 
reasonable approach based on the decreasing risk that enables the Company to lower the 
11 
overall cost of the LPP retirement program in the near term while still reaching the goals 
12 
of the program over the next two decades.  Exhibit __ (GIOP-4) provides projected leak 
13 
rates for LPP for various main replacement strategies. 
14

Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel

Q. 
Which programs contribute to retirement of LPP to meet the planned targets? 
1 
A. 
KEDLI’s Proactive Main Replacement Program is projected to replace approximately 95 
2 
percent of LPP mileage in the Rate Year and Data Years, with the remaining LPP 
3 
retirement mileage resulting from the CSC, Gas Reinforcement, and Gas Reliability 
4 
Programs. 
5 
 
6 
Q. 
How does the Company prioritize the retirement of main segments for the Proactive 
7 
Main Replacement Program? 
8 
A. 
Each year, the Company prioritizes retirement of LPP segments by using a risk ranking 
9 
algorithm that is part of the Company’s Distribution Integrity Management Plan (“DIMP”) 
10 
and the Company’s Gas Operating Procedure for the Identification, Evaluation and 
11 
Prioritization of Distribution Main Segments for Replacement (ENG04030).  The 
12 
Company’s risk model calculates a relative risk score for LPP segments based on specific 
13 
performance data and localized incident probabilities and consequences, combined with 
14 
calculated risk factors for the asset classes being evaluated.  This risk-based algorithm, 
15 
along with the Company’s sound engineering judgment, which takes all factors and risks 
16 
into consideration in each case, forms the foundation of the LPP retirement strategy.  The 
17 
Company is proposing to target more cast iron main for retirement where possible in 
18 
consideration of other risk factors, due to increased age and the type of material.  Flood 
19 
zone factors are also included in the risk ranking algorithm such that where other risk 
20 
factors are equal, the Company will target LPP retirements in Federal Emergency 
21 
Management Agency (“FEMA”) 100 and 500-year flood zones.

Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 35 of 105 
ii. 
Gas Safety Integrity Management and Integrity Verification Programs 
1 
Q. 
What is the Company’s IMP? 
2 
A. 
The Company’s transmission pipeline IMP is a safety program comprised of projects 
3 
mandated by the Pipeline Safety Improvement Act of 2002 and corresponding DOT 
4 
regulations.  The IMP identifies and addresses potential issues affecting the physical 
5 
soundness of Company facilities before they become safety or performance issues.  The 
6 
Company conducts baseline and periodic reassessments of transmission facilities to 
7 
identify and evaluate potential threats to “Covered Segments” of pipelines, i.e., 
8 
transmission pipelines that could affect “High Consequence Areas” (areas where a pipeline 
9 
failure could have significant adverse consequences), as well as remediation of significant 
10 
defects discovered during such assessments.  In regions of the United States where older 
11 
gas distribution systems are common, IMPs have become a key component of gas pipeline 
12 
safety through improving pipeline integrity. 
13 
 
14 
KEDLI’s IMP is designed to proactively address system risks identified through integrity 
15 
assessments in a manner that will also enable the Company to cost effectively comply with 
16 
existing and new IMP regulations. 
17 
 
18 
Q. 
Please describe the IMP capital investments. 
19 
A. 
Currently, IMP assessments may be accomplished through either external corrosion direct 
20 
assessment (“ECDA”) or in-line inspection (“ILI”) technology, which uses a device known 
21 
as a “pig” to evaluate the condition of the inside of the pipeline.  ILI is preferred to ECDA 
22 
because of the ability to identify condition anomalies originating in the pipe wall.  IMP 
23 


--- Page 38 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 36 of 105 
investments are necessary to: (i) support ILI (e.g., installation of pig launchers and 
1 
receivers, and pipe reconfiguration/replacement); and (ii) to resolve issues discovered 
2 
during pipeline inspections conducted through either method. 
3 
 
4 
Q. 
Please describe the IMP capital investment needs for the Rate Year and Data Years. 
5 
A. 
As of FY 2021, 16 percent of the Company’s DOT pipeline is ILI enabled.  If all projects 
6 
are completed within the Company’s current work plan, the plan will ultimately result in 
7 
20.4 percent of the Company’s DOT pipeline being ILI enabled by FY 2028.  This will 
8 
significantly improve the Company’s ability to identify integrity issues. 
9 
 
10 
Q. 
What is the status of federal regulations in this area? 
11 
A. 
In May 2016, PHMSA issued a Notice of Proposed Rulemaking (“May 2016 NPRM”) that 
12 
proposed new pipeline safety regulations including a requirement for increased inspection 
13 
of IMP-covered pipelines utilizing ILI technology and an expanded definition of Medium 
14 
Consequence Areas, as well as a host of other requirements.  To meet these requirements, 
15 
transmission pipelines must be ILI enabled, where possible.  The May 2016 NPRM was 
16 
adopted in a final rulemaking on October 1, 2019 (“2019 Final Rulemaking”), with an 
17 
effective date of July 1, 2020.  Because the Company’s IMP workplan is risk based, and in 
18 
light of PHMSA’s recent regulations expanding IMP, the Company believes that its 
19 
proposed IMP is a reasonable and compliant approach to managing pipeline integrity 
20 
during the Rate Year and Data Years. 
21 
 
22 
 
23 


--- Page 39 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 37 of 105 
Q. 
How are the IMP workplan and associated IMP capital investment forecast 
1 
determined? 
2 
A. 
The workplan began in 2002 based on initial assessments using the Company’s risk model.  
3 
Segments were prioritized for ILI enablement over a multi-year workplan based on a 
4 
combination of their risk score and other relevant factors, such as facility characteristics or 
5 
geography, to determine what work to do and the associated timing.  Segments must be re-
6 
assessed at a maximum of seven years according to the date of last assessment.  The 
7 
workplan is updated annually and as required assessments are completed.  Additionally, 
8 
ILI projects typically consist of three years of design and procurement work followed by 
9 
construction in the fourth year. 
10 
 
11 
IMP spending can fluctuate significantly year to year depending on the projects identified 
12 
for inclusion in the workplan.  Because the work must be done by segment, the length, 
13 
geographic location, and characteristics of the segments that need to be addressed each 
14 
year contribute to different costs such that historic program costs are not indicative of 
15 
future program needs.  Once segments that will be addressed by the workplan are 
16 
identified, cost forecasts, known as level one project estimates, are developed based on 
17 
prior projects of similar size and scope and adjusted for known variables.  Additionally, 
18 
some larger projects in the IMP are separately budgeted on the capital investment plan. 
19 
 
20 
The IMP workplan is described in more detail in the IMP data sheets in Exhibit __ (GIOP-
21 
5). 
22 
 
23 


--- Page 40 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 38 of 105 
Q. 
What is covered in the Company’s IVP Program? 
1 
A. 
The Pipeline Safety Act of 2011 also mandated that PHMSA establish rules requiring 
2 
operators to demonstrate their pipelines are “fit for service” by reviewing construction 
3 
records for each pipeline segment to confirm it is operating within design parameters.  On 
4 
January 4, 2011, PHMSA issued an advisory bulletin (ADB-11-01) directing operators to 
5 
perform a detailed threat and risk analysis that includes a records review of their systems.  
6 
The 2019 Final Rulemaking implemented additional rules regarding the maximum 
7 
allowable operating pressure (“MAOP”) and pressure testing requirements for existing 
8 
pipelines, including: (i) eliminating the exemption for establishing the MAOP of pre-1970 
9 
“grandfathered” pipe segments; (ii) mandating additional pressure testing or replacement 
10 
for pipelines without adequate pressure test records; and (iii) requiring operators who lack 
11 
certain records to establish material properties using approved methods (e.g., cutting and 
12 
testing pipe samples). 
13 
 
14 
The Company’s IVP program began in 2011 and includes thorough record reviews, 
15 
pipeline replacement, and retirement of non-essential pipeline segments.  As with the IMP, 
16 
the IVP is primarily based on the Company’s assessment of system risks, while also 
17 
incorporating PHMSA rulemakings. 
18 
 
19 
Q. 
What is the status of the Company’s IVP records review and its IVP proposal for the 
20 
Rate Year and Data Years? 
21 
A. 
Through its IVP to date, KEDLI has completed the MAOP records review on 100 percent 
22 
of its DOT-jurisdictional pipelines.  Going forward, the IVP addresses transmission main 
23 


--- Page 41 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 39 of 105 
replacements and testing necessitated by incomplete records identified by the review.  
1 
Where the Company has identified incomplete records, pipelines will be replaced, or 
2 
records will be recreated through testing. 
3 
 
4 
The Company’s proposed IVP work plan is comprised of several projects that will 
5 
incorporate elements of the 2019 Final Rulemaking and PHMSA guidance document 
6 
ADB-11-01 and will conduct records reviews, replace pipeline segments, retire non-
7 
essential pipeline segments, and perform critical engineering assessments. 
8 
 
9 
iii. 
Transmission Station Integrity Program 
10 
Q. 
Please describe the Transmission Station Integrity Program. 
11 
A. 
This program is similar to the Company’s IVP and consists of projects that address 
12 
transmission-connected pressure regulating stations and gate stations (“transmission 
13 
stations”).  The 2019 Final Rulemaking requires that operators: 
14 
 Confirm the records used to justify MAOP are traceable, verifiable, and complete; 
15 
 Reconfirm station MAOPs using assessment methods; 
16 
 Perform routine assessments on station piping in both High and Medium 
17 
Consequence Areas; 
18 
 Consider seismicity as a risk factor in its IMP; 
19 
 Perform material sampling to establish properties; and 
20 
 Advance fitness for service analysis. 
21 
 
22 
 
23 


--- Page 42 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 40 of 105 
Related advisory bulletins and discussions between PHMSA and industry stakeholders 
1 
during the May 2016 NPRM and 2019 Final Rulemaking process have indicated that the 
2 
proposed rules apply to transmission stations.  Therefore, the Company is proposing an 
3 
integrity verification program for records review and, where records are inadequate to 
4 
demonstrate that transmission station facilities are fit for service, capital investments for 
5 
station rehabilitation, or partial or full station replacements to bring the stations into 
6 
compliance.  Similar to the IMP and IVP for transmission pipelines, the Transmission 
7 
Station Integrity Program is prudent and in compliance with the 2019 Final Rulemaking to 
8 
address risk presented by older pressure regulating facilities for which there are inadequate 
9 
records. 
10 
 
11 
 
The Company began ramping up this program in fiscal year (“FY”) 2021, with a projected 
12 
completion date in FY 2035.  The program forecast is based on investment-grade estimates 
13 
to address the population of existing transmission stations that will be covered by the 
14 
program.

Q. 
Please describe some of the technologies and practices the Company uses to reduce 
7 
the total cost of its capital expenditures. 
8 
A. 
The Company continues to employ a number of technologies and best practices designed 
9 
to increase the efficiency and reduce the cost of its capital expenditures.  These practices 
10 
include: 
11 
 Increasing the amount of planned capital work (versus reactive work). 
12 
 Increasing coordination among capital programs to increase efficiencies (e.g., 
13 
leveraging LPP opportunities). 
14 
 Installing more small-diameter, high-pressure facilities that can be installed at 
15 
lower cost. 
16 
 Using smaller excavating equipment, increasing operating efficiency and reducing 
17 
instances of damage (because of decreased size and weight of equipment). 
18 
 Using “coring and keyhole” technology to repair existing mains. 
19 
 Enhancing contractor management. 
20 
 On-site reporting for work crews in many large construction projects. 
21 
 Continuing to deploy cured-in-place lining (as described in more detail below).

A. Capital Planning, Budgeting, and Sanctioning Process 
1 
Q. 
Please describe the annual development of the Company’s capital plan. 
2 
A. 
Each year, the Company develops a ten-year gas capital plan to achieve its performance 
3 
objectives of delivering safe and reliable service.  Investment Planning compiles proposed 
4 
spending for programs and individual capital projects.  Programs and projects are 
5 
categorized into one of the previously mentioned eight spending rationales (i.e. Mandated, 
6 
Reliability, Customer Connections, Non-Infrastructure, Advanced Communications 
7 
Infrastructure, Future of Heat, Operator Qualifications, and Indirects). 
8 
 
9 
In late fall, the capital plan is reviewed by the New York Jurisdictional President and the 
10 
New York Chief Financial Officer.  The New York Jurisdictional President reviews the 
11 
overall customer, service quality, and financial impacts of the investment plan as part of 
12 
the business planning process and may request changes to the level or mix of investments. 
13 
 
14 
In early winter, the capital plan is presented to the National Grid Board of Directors and 
15 
the National Grid plc Executive Committee and, in early spring, the capital portfolio is 
16 
presented to the National Grid plc Board of Directors for review and approval. 
17 
 
18 
Q. 
Are there additional approvals needed before a project in the annual capital plan may 
19 
proceed? 
20 
A. 
Yes.  Aside from the capital planning and budgeting process, specific delegation of 
21 
authority (“DOA”) approval must be obtained for any project in the ten-year capital plan 
22 
to proceed.  This process includes the sanctioning documentation and review for projects 
23 


--- Page 47 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 45 of 105 
over $2.5 million and other levels of review for smaller projects.  Presently, all projects 
1 
greater than $2.5 million require sanctioning documentation and review.  The New York 
2 
Executive Sanctioning Committee (“NYESC”) was established by the National Grid USA 
3 
Board of Directors specifically for this purpose.  Projects greater than $50 million are 
4 
reviewed and approved by the NYESC.  For projects between $2.5 million and $50 million, 
5 
the NYESC has delegated review to an informal committee led by the investment planning 
6 
group that reviews and finalizes sanctioning papers for these projects at a weekly meeting, 
7 
and the committee then forwards the final sanction documents to the executive sponsor of 
8 
the project for approval and signature.  Projects less than $2.5 million do not require 
9 
sanctioning and are approved through a supervisory DOA hierarchy based on certain 
10 
established thresholds. 
11 
 
12 
Q. 
Please explain the difference between the DOA review and approval (sanctioning) 
13 
process and the approved five-year capital plan used to forecast the Rate Year and 
14 
Data Years. 
15 
A. 
The timing of the sanctioning process is not aligned with the capital planning process used 
16 
to forecast the Rate Year and Data Years for purposes of the rate filing.  As described 
17 
above, the Company develops a long-term investment plan that is used as the basis for the 
18 
forecast for the Rate Year and Data Year proposals.  Project sanctioning, however, 
19 
normally occurs immediately prior to the fiscal year for which the investment is planned.  
20 
For example, the FY 2025 capital plan will be formally sanctioned in early 2024.  Thus, 
21 
the Company’s currently sanctioned or partially sanctioned projects do not yet represent 
22 
the full capital forecast proposed in the Rate Year and Data Years. 
23 


--- Page 48 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 46 of 105 
Q. 
Please describe how the Company’s DIMP impacts its capital investment planning. 
1 
A. 
The DIMP involves a risk-based assessment of the Company’s distribution system to 
2 
identify threats in seven categories: corrosion, natural forces, excavation damage, other 
3 
outside force damage, material and weld failure, equipment failure/malfunction, and 
4 
inappropriate operation.  The DIMP requires evaluation and prioritization of the risks that 
5 
these threats pose, and the implementation of measures to address the highest risks with an 
6 
emphasis on leak management, enhanced damage prevention, operator qualification to 
7 
reduce human error and system replacement.  Consistent with the DIMP, the Company 
8 
prioritizes asset replacements in its investment plan based on a risk ranking that considers, 
9 
among other things, leak repair history, types of leak, pipe material, surrounding 
10 
geography, segment length, nearby construction activity, field conditions, customer issues, 
11 
open leaks, and engineering judgment.  The risk ranking factors are carefully designed to 
12 
consider known differences in the performance of asset subclasses, extensive experience 
13 
with asset failures, current performance data for the asset subclasses for various threat 
14 
categories, and subject matter experts’ analysis and opinions on the future performance of 
15 
the assets. 
16 
 
17 
B. Mandated Category of Capital Spending 
18 
Q. 
What portion of the Company’s capital investment plan is Mandated? 
19 
A. 
The Mandated category of work accounts for approximately 73 percent ($484.346 million) 
20 
of the total planned direct capital investment in the Rate Year. 
21 
 
22 
 
23 


--- Page 49 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 47 of 105 
Q. 
Please describe what is included in the Mandated spending category. 
1 
A. 
Projects covered by the Mandated spending rationale are those needed to comply with 
2 
regulatory obligations and rate plan commitments, including: the CSC Program that 
3 
requires the Company to relocate facilities, code-required corrosion testing and mitigation, 
4 
pipeline and station integrity related activity (including IMP and IVP as described above), 
5 
proactive and reactive capital main and service replacement, and required meter 
6 
replacements.  Exhibit __ (GIOP-5) includes summary descriptions of the larger projects 
7 
included in the Company’s Mandated spending rationale/category, along with the 
8 
estimated annual funding during the Rate Year and Data Years. 
9 
 
10 
Q. 
Please describe what is included in the CSC program. 
11 
A. 
This program consists of construction work performed to accommodate third-party, 
12 
municipal construction activity that could impact the integrity of the Company’s natural 
13 
gas facilities.  Typical third-party construction activities that impact gas facilities include 
14 
work on water, sewer and drainage infrastructure, street reconstruction, road realignment 
15 
and bridge replacement.  State regulations and Company procedures require the 
16 
replacement of eight inch and smaller cast iron gas mains if roadway or underground 
17 
construction is being performed in such a way that would impact the integrity of the 
18 
Company’s mains.  Non-cast iron gas mains (i.e., steel and plastic) are not subject to the 
19 
same replacement regulations and are typically supported and protected if not in direct 
20 
conflict with third-party construction.  Direct conflicts are addressed through relocation 
21 
regardless of material type. 
22

Q. 
Please describe what is included in the Valve Installation and Replacement Program. 
1 
A. 
Federal and state regulations require installation, inspection, operation, and maintenance 
2 
of critical pipeline valves on all gas distribution systems.  The purpose of these valves is to 
3 
facilitate the rapid shutdown of distribution piping during gas emergencies such as third-
4 
party damage or water intrusion.  A secondary purpose of these valves is to facilitate 
5 
maintenance and pipe replacement on associated distribution piping. 
6 
 
7 
This program will enhance the Company’s emergency response capabilities by improving 
8 
the level at which Field Operations personnel can safely and efficiently isolate sections of 
9 
the distribution system while mitigating customer impacts (e.g., reducing the duration of 
10 
future outages).  Ensuring all valves are properly maintained and operable is a critical 
11 
public safety function and is essential to the effective operation of the Company’s gas 
12 
distribution system.  The Rate Year forecast of costs for the program is approximately 
13 
$0.011 million each year, adjusted for inflation in each of the Data Years. 
14 
 
15 
Q. 
Please describe what is included in the Gas Remote Controlled Valve (“RCV”) 
16 
projects. 
17 
A. 
These capital projects involve the installation of additional RCVs on transmission pipelines 
18 
to improve emergency response capability and reduce the risk of gas releases.  In the event 
19 
of a pipeline failure, RCVs allow control room operators to stop the flow of gas and to 
20 
remotely isolate and shut down a portion of the system.  Currently, most transmission 
21 
pipelines can only be shut down using manually operated isolation valves, which can take 
22 
longer to close and result in a larger customer impact.  Improving response time through 
23 


--- Page 56 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 54 of 105 
the expanded deployment of RCVs reduces the quantity of gas released and can limit the 
1 
harm to the public and property. 
2 
 
3 
Q. 
Why does the Company propose to include installation of RCVs in the capital 
4 
investment plan? 
5 
A. 
Recent PHMSA regulations promulgated in response to the Pipeline Safety Act of 2011 
6 
mandate the installation of system valves.  As highlighted by recent industry events, there 
7 
are significant operational benefits associated with the increased deployment of rupture 
8 
mitigation valves and RCVs, such as enhanced pipeline shutdown capabilities.  Mandated 
9 
investment in these valves also provides system safety and reliability benefits and is an 
10 
industry best practice. 
11 
 
12

9

10

13 
Q. 
Please describe what is included in the Heater Installation Program. 
14 
A. 
There are 31 natural gas heaters currently operating on the Company’s system.  Because 
15 
high-pressure gas cools when reduced to a lower pressure, heaters are required at some 
16 
pressure regulating stations to prevent freeze-ups that can impact flow control devices.  In 
17 
addition, cold gas temperatures can lead to reduced pipe toughness and increased potential 
18 
for frost heave and cold temperature-induced stresses.  The heater program adds new 
19 
heaters (where required) and replaces existing heaters that have reached the end of their 
20 
useful lives. 
21 
 
22 
 
23 


--- Page 59 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 57 of 105 
The investments in the Heater Installation Program do not cover the special project capital 
1

2 
described in Exhibit __ (GIOP-5). 
3 
 
4 
Q. 
Please describe what is included in the Proactive Regulator Stations Program. 
5 
A. 
The Proactive Regulator Stations Program provides annual funding for replacement and/or 
6 
rebuilding and reconditioning of existing regulating and control stations.  Pressure 
7 
regulating facilities (or stations) are designed to control system pressures and maintain 
8 
continuous and reliable supply under all operating conditions. 
9 
 
10 
KEDLI conducts asset condition assessments at all pressure regulating stations on its 
11 
system every three years, evaluating factors such as pressure, location, including whether 
12 
the station is located in a FEMA 100-year or 500-year flood zone, and the number of 
13 
dependent customers for each station.  In addition, the assessment considers station 
14 
condition, including pipe corrosion, location and type of overpressure protection, 
15 
automation, condition of vaults, vault covers, wall sleeves, piping vents and ladders.  The 
16 
results of the assessment are used to create an overall risk rating for each station that serves 
17 
as the basis for prioritizing projects in this program.  The program includes full or partial 
18 
replacement of existing stations, and storm hardening upgrades where necessary. 
19 
 
20 
The investments in the Proactive Regulator Stations Program do not cover the special 
21 
project capital improvements to specific stations that are separately set forth in the 
22 
investment plan. 
23

Q.

1 
A.

Q. 
Please describe what is included in the Gas Reliability – System Automation category. 
20 
A. 
The Gas Reliability – System Automation Program involves the installation of Remote 
21 
Terminal Units (“RTUs”) at multiple city gate and regulator stations.  RTUs provide 
22 
temperature, pressure, and flow data back to the gas control room.  RTUs can also monitor 
23 


--- Page 62 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 60 of 105 
gas detectors and intrusion alarms and allow gas control operators to adjust flow and 
1 
pressure set points at regulator stations.  The benefits include enhanced calibration of 
2 
network models from automation and telemetry data, improved accuracy of network 
3 
analysis, and enhanced ability to forecast the need for capital reinforcements, which will 
4 
lead to more efficient capital planning.  Automation allows gas control operators to 
5 
selectively close valves, raise or lower pressures, and shut down take stations.  System 
6 
alarms also alert gas control operators to system issues and allow operators to quickly 
7 
pinpoint the source of the alarm. 
8 
PHMSA regulations regarding Control Room Management require operators to ensure that 
9 
“practices and procedures within their control rooms are adequate to maintain pipeline 
10 
safety and integrity.”  These rules indicate that operators should have telemetry to monitor 
11 
pipelines, as it would increase system awareness and enable a proactive response to 
12 
abnormal operating conditions.  The Gas Reliability – System Automation category 
13 
complies with these regulations by increasing deployment of telemetry on the Company’s 
14 
system. 
15 
 
16 
Q. 
How is system performance monitored currently? 
17 
A. 
Currently, KEDLI’s pressure regulation stations are equipped with some form of telemetry, 
18 
with 96 percent of the system having full remote control of the regulators.  Additionally, 
19 
some of the telemetry equipment was installed years ago and the technology has become 
20 
obsolete.  KEDLI’s Gas Reliability – System Automation Program adds automation 
21 
capabilities to low pressure regulator stations and updates RTU equipment and 
22 


--- Page 63 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 61 of 105 
communications capabilities.  This program will provide enhanced ability to monitor 
1 
system performance and remotely adjust pressures on the gas system. 
2 
 
3 
Q. 
Please describe what is included in the Main Upgrades – Water Intrusion Program. 
4 
A. 
The Main Upgrades – Water Intrusion Program is designed to address water entering the 
5 
gas distribution system, resulting in main obstructions, poor pressure and/or freezing 
6 
customer services.  This program targets the retirement of LPP that is susceptible to water 
7 
intrusion but is not prioritized for replacement under the Main Replacement programs 
8 
because of the absence of leaks and/or historical leak repair activity. 
9 
 
10 
Q. 
Please describe the Storm Hardening – Remote Service Shutoff Valves Program. 
11 
A. 
The Storm Hardening – Remote Service Shutoff Valves Program installs smart meters with 
12 
integrated shutoff valves on gas services within FEMA’s designated flood zones.  Remote 
13 
shutoff valves add a layer of protection for services that are especially susceptible to storm 
14 
surges and flooding.  The impacts of past severe storms (i.e., Superstorm Sandy and 
15 
Hurricane Irene) reinforce the need for the Company to harden its infrastructure to provide 
16 
greater protection from future storms.  These valves will operate on a fixed communication 
17 
network that will allow for remote operation and monitoring.  Automated valves stop the 
18 
flow of gas as soon as flooding is detected.  This will prevent regulator over-pressurization 
19 
and stop gas from flowing to premises with damaged equipment and/or extinguished pilot 
20 
lights, mitigating the risk of a potential incident.  Automated valves also provide a real-
21 
time count of services impacted by flooding to inform the Company’s storm response about 
22 
the resources needed to restore the affected customers expeditiously.  Lastly, in areas where 
23 


--- Page 64 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 62 of 105 
flooding prevents physical access to valves and regulators, remote shut-off valves will 
1 
allow the Company to interrupt only those services impacted by flooding, which could 
2 
prevent entire neighborhoods or larger areas from losing gas service because of access 
3 
issues. 
4 
 
5 
The Company intends to install 2,500 valves annually in each of the Rate Year, Data Year 
6 
1, and Data Year 2.  In Data Year 3, the program will install approximately 5,000 ultrasonic 
7 
smart meters with integrated shutoff valves.  The Company has scheduled this program in 
8 
Data Year 3 to allow for deployment of the capital investments in the Advanced 
9 
Communications Infrastructure capital category and will leverage the communication 
10 
system established in that category to interconnect the smart meters to maximize the 
11 
benefits of this program for the Company and its customers.  Program costs are 
12 
approximately $3.988 million in the Rate Year, $4.059 million in Data Year 1, $4.220 
13 
million in Data Year 2, and $4.337 million in Data Year 3.  This program will require eight 
14 
additional FTEs in Data Year 3 to oversee system monitoring and valve location support, 
15 
investigate alarms, and replace valve components, as necessary. 
16 
 
17 
Q. 
Please describe the Transmission Service Integrity Program. 
18 
A. 
Currently, individual transmission customers are fed directly from Company-owned and 
19 
operated transmission facilities, including piping, regulator stations, and metering, before 
20 
the service is attached to customer-owned piping.  This program will perform a risk 
21 
assessment of the Company-owned transmission facilities against current National Grid 
22 
standards, develop a maintenance schedule, and perform any work deemed necessary by 
23 


--- Page 65 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 63 of 105 
the risk assessment.  Completion of the risk assessment and associated remedial work will 
1 
increase the safety and reliability of these transmission assets and prevent against the 
2 
development of leaks on these facilities.  The initial risk assessment and work plan required 
3 
for these services will be developed in FY 2024, and the associated work plan will be 
4 
completed in Data Years 2 and 3 at a cost of $4.000 million annually. 
5 
 
6 
Q. 
Please describe the Regulator Station Over Pressure Protection Program. 
7 
A. 
As exhibited by the Merrimack Valley incident, preventing over pressurization is vital for 
8 
the safe operation of the Company’s gas system.  This program will install additional and 
9 
replace obsolete protective devices at regulator stations on the Company’s system to reduce 
10 
the risk of over pressurization.  Specifically, this program will install a third layer of 
11 
protection, override pilots, at regulator stations where external control lines are susceptible 
12 
to third-party damage or isolation that could result in an over pressure event.  In addition, 
13 
as part of the program, the Company will replace antiquated oil seal relief devices with 
14 
modern mechanical relief devices to deliver increased reliability and functionality.  In the 
15 
event of an over pressurization, the operation of a mechanical relief device provides 
16 
reductions in carbon emissions as compared to the oil seal and will eliminate the possibility 
17 
of oil contamination.  The Company began installation of override pilots in FY 2021, and 
18 
the program will continue until all stations are addressed.  The capital investment required 
19

17

21

22

23

refill the tank.  The Holtsville LNG Plant is a cost-effective means of meeting peak demand 
1

2 
lower and stored for use in the winter during peak demand conditions.

5

7

8

9

10

11 
KEDLI is projected to come from new construction, which is only minimally impacted by 
12

Q. 
Please describe the Gas System Reinforcement Program in the Customer Connections 
15 
category of spending. 
16 
A. 
The Gas System Reinforcement Program contains projects intended to ensure that 
17 
minimum system pressures are maintained throughout the gas network during periods of 
18 
peak demand.  The Company models peak demand based on the sendout forecasts 
19 
developed by the Economics and Load Forecasting group (see direct testimony of the Gas 
20 
Load Forecasting Panel).  As a result of demand for gas usage in its service territory, 
21 
KEDLI has determined that it is necessary to complete a number of projects to ensure its 
22 
ability to meet peak requirements.  These reinforcement projects are essential to ensure the 
23 


--- Page 72 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 70 of 105 
Company can continue to serve growing gas usage load.  Without such reinforcements, the 
1 
Company estimates that more than 10,000 existing customers could experience loss of 
2 
service. 
3 
 
4 
Over the past five years, KEDLI has experienced six top-ten records for sendout during the 
5 
respective winter heating seasons.  During the winter of 2018/2019, KEDLI recorded its 
6 
highest sendout, a firm load record of 1,029,616 Dth on January 21, 2019, when the average 
7 
temperature was thirteen degrees Fahrenheit.  KEDLI recorded its most recent and fifth 
8 
highest sendout record on February 3, 2023, with a firm load of 952,651 Dth and an average 
9 
temperature of thirteen degrees Fahrenheit, which indicates that the current demand levels 
10 
in the KEDLI territory remain strong. 
11 
 
12 
Q. 
Please provide examples of System Reinforcement projects. 
13 
A. 
Examples of System Reinforcement projects include: 
14 
 Replacing undersized mains with larger diameter mains.  LPP is targeted whenever 
15 
practical during this work. 
16 
 Looping or connecting system endpoints by installing new main. 
17 
 System pressure uprates (e.g., 10 pounds per square inch (“psi”) to 60 psi). 
18 
 Installing new district regulators and replacing existing undersized district 
19 
regulators. 
20 
 Transferring existing low-pressure customers to an adjacent high-pressure main 
21 
(i.e., load shedding). 
22

18

--- Page 77 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 75 of 105 
Q. 
Please describe the equipment and systems comprising a RNG interconnection. 
1 
A. 
A RNG interconnection system conditions methane gas generated from biomass to meet 
2 
pipeline quality for direct injection into the natural gas distribution system using the 
3 
following components and systems: 
4 
 Gas Quality – The Company requires gas chromatographs and sulfur/moisture 
5 
analyzers to monitor gas delivered from an RNG conditioning system to ensure RNG 
6 
meets gas quality requirements.  In any instance where out-of-specification gas is 
7 
measured, an actuated flow control valve would be closed to prevent injection into the 
8 
system. 
9 
 Pressure Regulation – The Company requires pressure to be regulated from higher 
10 
pressure down to the MAOP of the gas network.  Equally important, a third layer of 
11 
protection to avoid over pressurization will be installed at the RNG interconnection to 
12 
prevent the assets from exceeding their maximum allowable operating pressure. 
13 
 Odorization – For health and safety purposes, odorant must be incorporated into on-
14 
system gas supplies to alert the public of gas leaks. 
15 
 Sales Meter / Control Room – The Company’s Gas Operations team needs real-time 
16 
data about production from any RNG facility. 
17 
 Piping and Valving – RNG interconnections connect a conditioning system to enable 
18 
injection into the Company’s natural gas distribution system. 
19 
 
20 
The Northeast Gas Association developed the Interconnect Guide for Renewable Natural 
21 
Gas (RNG) in New York State to provide technical guidance necessary for introducing RNG 
22 
into the natural gas distribution system that can be found at: 
23 


--- Page 78 ---
Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 76 of 105 
https://www.northeastgas.org/pdf/nga_gti_interconnect_0919.pdf. 
1 
 
2

Please describe the proposed RNG Interconnections. 
3 
A. 
The Company is proposing two RNG interconnections: a wastewater treatment plant in 
4 
Nassau County and a food waste-to-RNG facility in Suffolk County. 
5 
 
6 
RNG Interconnection 1 
7 
The Company is working with the owners of a wastewater treatment plant in Nassau 
8 
County to inject RNG into the Company’s adjacent distribution network.  The project will 
9 
reduce the treatment plant’s carbon footprint and associated emissions and minimize 
10 
impacts of GHG emissions to the local community.  There is already a joint venture 
11 
consulting team in place to evaluate design option, and the project is expected to produce 
12 
approximately 450 Dth/day. 
13 
 
14 
The projected capital costs for the interconnection are $5.305 million and $0.166 million 
15 
for approximately 1,000 feet of pipe, for a total estimated cost of $5.471 million.

RNG Interconnection 2 
18
