Work Description:  
NL-15 & NL-16 are located at Hempstead, New York and are 1959 vintage stations consisting of aboveground safety and 
district regulator. The inlet pressure to the stations is 350 psig and the outlet pressure of the stations is 99 psig.  NL-15/NL-16 
are a two-stage station consisting of two (2) 6” Fisher 1098H EGR/30 in series and no third layer of Over Pressure Protection 
(OPP).  Loss of the two stations could potentially impact thousands of customers.  NL-16 is located adjacent to NL-15 station 
both projects will be developed at the same time.  
 
Work Scope for both stations will include:  
•

•

•

available for the station.  When adequate records are not available, National Grid has decided it is most prudent to replace 
pipes and equipment that are missing required documentation. Under the current PHMSA ruling, this work must be 
completed by July 1, 2035. 
 
Customer Benefits:

Transmission Station Integrity - PHMSA - Northport - S-326 
 
 
 
 
Type: ☒ Project ☐ Program 
Category: ☒ Capital  ☐ Both Capital & O&M 
 
Investment Code: 5230101585  
Region: ☐ KEDNY ☒ KEDLI  
Planning Portfolio: ☐ Customer Connections ☒ Mandated ☐ Reliability ☐ Non-Infrastructure ☐ Indirect 
Annual Program: ☐ 
Estimated Start Date: 01/01/2026 
Estimated In-Service Date: 11/30/2026 
Sponsor: Leyble, Dennis  
 
Business Contact: Adesina, Risikat  
 
 
Work Description:  
S-326 is a 1992 vintage station that provides service to a power station on Long Island, New York.  The station has an inlet 
pressure of 1440 psi and has an outlet pressure of 300 psi.  S-326 consists of one 2” Fisher EZ at the primary stage regulator, 
one 2” Fisher V-Ball secondary stage regulator, and no third layer over pressurization protection (OPP).  Due to the current 
condition of the station, a full station rebuild including the following work scope is required:  
 
Work Scope: 
• 
Installation of a new station inlet valve, station outlet valve, strainer, primary regulator, secondary regulator, and OPP 
• 
Installation of new station piping and control lines 
• 
Installation of new vent poles  
• 
Installation of new access manholes and ladder systems 
• 
Installation of pipe supports within each vault 
 
Justification Summary:  
Due to recent PHMSA rule changes, a record review was performed at all regulating stations that fall under the new guidelines.  
The record review was performed to ensure both material and hydrostatic test records were accurate and available for the 
station.  When adequate records are not available, National Grid has decided it is most prudent to replace pipes and 
equipment that are missing required documentation.  Under the current PHMSA ruling, this work must be completed by July 
1, 2035. 
 
Customer Benefits: 
The current max flow through the station is 18,608 MCFH.  This project will bring S-326 service station into compliance with 
the PHMSA ruling and improve system reliability.

Work Description:  
S-344 is a 1998 vintage station that provides service to a power station in Long Island, New York. The station has an inlet

stage regulator, one 8” Fisher EZH as the secondary stage regulator, and a third layer of over pressurization protection (OPP) 
in the form of a 6” Fisher 414 RV regulator. Due to the current condition of the station, a full station rebuild including the

Transmission Station Integrity - PHMSA - Rockville 
Centre Take Station Overhaul 
 
Type: ☒ Project ☐ Program 
Category: ☒ Capital  ☐ Both Capital & O&M 
 
Investment Code: 5230101599 
Region: ☐ KEDNY ☒ KEDLI

Sponsor: Leyble, Dennis  
 
Business Contact: Adesina, Risikat  
 
Work Description:  
Rockville Centre Gate Station is in Lynbrook, New York and consists of three (3) regulating stations being QL-18, QL-19, and 
QL-20.  QL-18, 19, and 20 are 1969 vintage regulating stations consisting of a primary regulator vault, a secondary regulator 
vault, and a metering vault.  The inlet pressure to all three regulator stations is 450 psi with QL-18, 19 outlet pressure of 99 
psi and QL-20 outlet pressure of 60 psi.  QL-18 feeds the Southshore 99# system, QL-19 feeds the Lakeview 99# system and 
QL-20 feeds the Inwood 60# system.  Due to missing DOT pipe records, a full replacement of all DOT piping within the station 
is required.  Additionally, due to the current condition of the station, a full station rebuild is called for consisting of the 
following work scope: 
  
Work Scope:    
• 
Installation of a new station inlet valve, station outlet valve, strainer, primary regulator, secondary regulator, and OPP 
• 
Installation of new station piping and control lines 
• 
Installation of new vent poles  
• 
Installation of new access manholes and ladder systems 
• 
Installation of pipe supports within each vault 
 
Justification Summary:  
Due to recent PHMSA rule changes, are record review was performed at all regulating stations that fall under the new 
guidelines.  The record review was performed to ensure both material and hydrostatic test records were accurate and 
available for the station.  When adequate records are not available, National Grid has decided it is most prudent to replace 
pipes and equipment that are missing required documentation. Under the current PHMSA ruling, this work must be 
completed by July 1, 2035. 
 
Customer Benefits: 
The current max flow through QL-18 is 960.37 MCFH. The current max flow through QL-19 is 940.91 MCFH. The current max 
flow through QL-20 is 1,609.72 MCFH.  The completion of this project will bring National Grid into compliance with PHMSA 
ruling and improve system reliability.

Transmission Station Integrity - PHMSA 
Type: ☐ Project ☒ Program
Category: ☒ Capital  ☐ Both Capital & O&M
Investment Code: 5230001961 
Region: ☐ KEDNY ☒ KEDLI
Planning Portfolio: ☐ Customer Connections ☒ Mandated ☐ Reliability ☐ Non-Infrastructure ☐ Indirect
Annual Program: ☒
Estimated Start Date: 
Estimated In-Service Date: 
Sponsor: Leyble, Dennis 
Business Contact: Adesina, Risikat 
Work Description: 
This program will be used to fund annual capital improvement projects to applicable regulating stations across Long Island. 
Work will include the replacement of station equipment to ensure compliance with new PHMSA rulings that require the 
maximum allowable operating pressure (MAOP) of all regulating station equipment from the station inlet valve to the station 
outlet valve to be confirmed through records that are traceable, verifiable, and complete (TVC). 
Justification Summary: 
Due to recent PHMSA rule changes, are record review was performed at all regulating stations that fall under the new 
guidelines.  The record review was performed to ensure both material and hydrostatic test records were accurate and 
available for the station.  When adequate records are not available, National Grid has decided it is most prudent to replace 
pipes and equipment that are missing required documentation.  Under the current PHMSA ruling, this work must be 
completed by July 1, 2035. 
Customer Benefits: 
This capital improvement program encompasses various transmission stations within the KEDLI distribution system and has 
the potential of impacting system reliability for a wide range of customers throughout Long Island.   
Funding Detail 
Cost Breakdown: 
No funding is allocated in FY25 because individual PHMSA projects have already been determined.

Transmission Station Integrity - PHMSA - SL-55 
Work Description: 
SL-55 is located at Ronkonkoma, New York and is a 1977 vintage station consisting of primary and secondary regulator vaults. 
The inlet pressure to the station is 350 psi and the outlet pressure of the station is 124 psi.  The station consists of 6" Fisher 
1098H-EGR/30H regulator in the primary vault and 6" Fisher 1098H-EGR/30 regulator in the secondary vault.  Currently, there 
is no over pressurization protection (OPP) installed within the station. 
Work Scope will include: 
•
Relocate station to location less prone to vehicular damage
•
Installation of new dual run station piping from the station inlet valve to the station outlet valve
•
Installation of new primary, secondary and OPP vaults
•
Installation of new station inlet and outlet valves, regulators, and addition of OPP
•
Installation of a new strainer
•
Installation of Roxtec seals in all through wall penetrations
•
Installation of Bilco doors and new vent poles
•
Installation of new telemetry and instrumentation control equipment
•
Tie-in to existing transmission and distribution mains
Justification Summary: 
Due to recent PHMSA rule changes, are record review was performed at all regulating stations that fall under the new 
guidelines.  The record review was performed to ensure both material and hydrostatic test records were accurate and 
available for the station.  When adequate records are not available, National Grid has decided it is most prudent to replace 
pipes and equipment that are missing required documentation.  Under the current PHMSA ruling, this work must be 
completed by July 1, 2035. 
Current condition of the station: 
•
Atmospheric corrosion was observed on all piping and regulator components.
•
No adequate pipe supports are installed.
•
Dresser couplings were observed in both safety and district vaults.
•
Telemetry cabinets are mounted on the pole. Lines/elbows are corroded.
•
Drip pot is installed at the inlet to the station.
•
No third layer of protection is installed.
•
The station has never had cathodic protection. No insulating joints installed.

The current max flow through the station is 1102.716 MCFH.  Rebuilding the regulating station will improve the asset 
condition and safety, and address any open reliability concerns.  A third layer of overpressure protection will be added to 
protect the station in an emergency.

Business Contact: Steven Doben 
Initiative Title: Maintenance and Repair Investments 
 
Brief Description:   
 
The Company’s maintenance and repair investments in its facilities address break-fix and safety-
related work required to ensure continued delivery of safe and reliable service to customers and a 
safe working environment for the Company’s employees.   
 
Investments seek to address roofing failures, arc flash signage, life safety/fire alarm systems, and 
other general building improvements. Roofing systems will be repaired or replaced where water 
penetration has been identified, arc flash signage installed where required, life safety and fire alarm 
systems will be upgraded or replaced to code, and bathrooms renovated for ADA compliance.   
 
The following are sought as part of the Company’s maintenance and repair investments: 
 
Roof Replacements – Replace failed roofs across Company’s facilities to prevent water seepage 
that can cause severe and potentially catastrophic deterioration of building infrastructure, and to 
maintain structural integrity for the safety of employees and contractors. 
 
Bathroom Program – Upgrade Bathrooms for ADA compliance across the Company’s facilities 
footprint to improve public, contractor, and employee morale and safety, as well as employee 
retention. The investment will address code and compliance violations and fines from state or local 
code enforcement agencies. 
 
Arc Flash – Spending to address electrical panel and service marking updates required by the 
implementation of a new national electrical code standard to ensure safer identification and 
awareness of higher voltage electrical connection and/or controls. 
 
Life Safety and Fire Alarm Upgrades – Many of the Company’s life safety and fire alarm systems 
are old, outdated, and at end-of-life. Systems across the Company’s footprint must be upgraded to 
protect public and employee safety and to avoid violations from Long Island Fire Marshalls and 
other state and local code enforcement agencies. 
 
Rationale / Justification:  
 
End-of-life or failing equipment replacement and other upgrades are required to ensure a safe 
working environment and to preserve the integrity of the Company’s facilities. This will provide 
improved working conditions for employees, contractors, partners, and the public.   
 
• Customer – Proposed investments will ensure continued ability to provide safe and reliable 
service, without impact to operations or field force initiatives; 
• Economic – The proposed investments will address deferred maintenance and help address 
compliance with safety requirements and regulations, including the ADA.

improvements are deferred.

Initiative Title: Asset Refurbishment 
 
Brief Description:   
 
Asset Refurbishment investments are proposed to repair, replace, or restore broken and 
deteriorating building structures, and to address critical building systems in need of repair at the 
Company’s Melville site, among others. At Melville, investments will target end of life building 
systems like HVAC units and electrical and plumbing systems, as well as interior finishes in need 
of replacement or repair. Overall, asset refurbishment work seeks to address deferred maintenance 
and improve facilities that are essential to ensure the delivery of safe and reliable service to 
customers.  
 
Rationale / Justification:  
 
The investments in asset refurbishment are based on independently prepared facility condition 
reports, break-fix analysis, infrastructure deficiencies discovered during in-flight work, and from 
internal and external inspections. End of life, failing equipment replacement, and system upgrades 
are required, as well as corrections to building structures, to ensure safety, integrity and continued 
viability of noted sites. This will provide improved and safe working conditions for employees, 
contractors, partners, and the public. 
 
• Customer – Proposed investments will ensure continued ability to provide safe and reliable 
service to customers, without impact to operations or field force initiatives; 
• Economic – Investment proposed will address deferred maintenance and avoid total 
building and/or building system failure, which exceed the cost of repairs

Business Contact: Steven Doben 
Initiative Title: Riverhead Building 
 
Brief Description:   
 
The Riverhead Building project is a planned and in-flight $10m capital investment in a new 
ground-up building being constructed at 43 Doctor’s Path in Riverhead, NY. The Company-owned 
parcel where the new building will be sited adjoins the location of a current operating facility that 
it will replace. The current facility is co-located with LiPA / PSEG and, because of that, presents 
operational challenges. The new location is anticipated to be approximately 7,500sf and will 
support 20+/- employees supporting the Company’s gas business, as well as parking, material 
stock and storage, and a fleet repair station. This project will enable the full separation of the 
facility from the Company’s primary tenant and, as a result, will enable a potential sale of the 
current site. 
 
Development costs are based on ongoing projects underway by the Company in KEDLI territory, 
as well as preliminary conceptual plans for new site construction projects.  Energy efficient 
solutions and sustainable materials, systems, and building practices will be incorporated where 
practicable.  
Rationale / Justification:  
A separate, stand-alone site will improve operational control, efficiency, and communications, 
improving customer response times, interaction, and service.  Executive and employee teams have 
indicated that a separate, fully segmented site also supports improved focus, collaboration, and 
workforce attraction, retention, and development.  
Program / Investment Budget:

Alternatives:

separation to support operational efficiency. A decision to pursue the separation was 
deemed essential to ensuring the Company’s continued ability to effectively serve its 
customers, and to also provide a model for separation at other similarly co-located sites.  
 
2. Defer the Project – The challenges presented to Company employees by co-locating at 
this site and the opportunity to develop a new one on Company-owned land on an adjacent 
parcel, combined, present a solution that is both beneficial to the Company and customers.

Business Contact: Steven Doben 
Initiative Title: Long Island Warehouse 
 
Brief Description:   
 
The Long Island Warehouse project is a planned and in flight $4m capital fit out of a new, built to 
suit 100,000sf warehouse that will be leased and used to support the Company’s gas operations 
warehousing requirements.  It is anticipated that this site will also require an additional 20 FTE’s.  
It will house materials currently stored at National Grid’s Greenpoint campus.  The new site will 
provide local, immediate access to materials for all gas groups (C&M, Complex Construction, 
CMS) in Long Island. As presented, it will be leased for an initial 10-year term with two 5-year 
renewal options and an initial annual rent of $2.1m with 3% annual escalations. The FTE cost (not 
fully loaded) is estimated at $1.2m annually. 
  
Development costs are based on ongoing projects underway by the Company, as well as 
preliminary conceptual plans for new site construction projects. Energy efficient solutions and 
sustainable materials, systems, and building practices will be incorporated, where practicable.  
 
Rationale / Justification:  
The current warehouse operation at Greenpoint will continue to support the KEDNY business and 
its customers. With the changes in the Company’s supply chain strategy to be supported in-house 
vs third party materials storage, plus continued storage needs for KEDLI’s gas operations and 
network, the sharing of a single site is no longer adequate and presents risk to material availability 
and the Company’s ability to meet timing commitments.  The Long Island Warehouse facility 
seeks to address KEDLI’s operational needs without reliance on stock at the Greenpoint facility.

Island warehouse location was deemed essential to minimize logistical costs and ensure 
the Company’s continued ability to effectively provide customers with safe and reliable 
service in KEDLI territory.

Initiative Title: Operator Qualification Training Facility 
 
Brief Description:   
 
The Operator Qualification Training Facility project is an investment in a new facility to support 
recently enacted regulatory requirements associated with gas facility operator qualifications. The 
site will be a mix of classroom and hands-on training space that will include necessary equipment, 
and associated parking and amenities.   
Rationale / Justification:  
Gas Safety regulations require Operator Qualification training for new and existing employees of 
the Company responsible for performing certain pipeline and other gas-related activities, as 
governed by both the PHMSA regulations (49 CFR § 192.805) and the Commission’s gas safety 
regulations (16 NYCRR Part 255).   
This investment will support the new training requirements, enacted by the Public Service 
Commission in 2022, and ensure that the Company is able to meet them in a timely manner.

existing training sites is not recommended.  
 
2. Defer the Project – In order to ensure that the Company’s training facility is adequate to 
meet the expanded requirements as soon as possible, consistent with the Public Service 
Commission’s recent changes to the gas safety regulations to meet regulatory obligation.

Initiative Title: I&R Pressure Training Facility

Business Contact: Therese Sullivan 
Initiative Title: Fleet EV Chargers and Make-Ready Work 
Brief Description: 
 
As part of the Company’s initiative to electrify its fleet, it is seeking investment in electric vehicle 
(EV) charging and associated make-ready work sufficient to meet its proposed fleet electrification 
schedule. This charger investment is two-pronged and includes: (1) the EV charging units and 
related facilities being installed at various Company locations as a capital investment, as needed; 
and (2) infrastructure improvements, or “make-ready” work, needed to bring electric service to 
required Company locations where EVs are being sought to meet charger electricity load 
requirements, as an operational expense1.  
 
Infrastructure improvements will be done in conjunction and coordination with electric utilities 
serving Company facilities. Those efforts are underway but require detailed design and load 
requirements to advance. EV chargers and associated facilities will be purchased by vendors at a 
pace that ensures EVs can continue to meet existing operational need without disruption to the 
implementation timeline.  
 
By initiating make-ready work and EV charger installations before EVs replace internal 
combustion engine (ICE) vehicles reaching end of life, the Company expects that the benefits EVs 
add to meeting GHG emissions reduction targets under the CLCPA can occur without interrupting 
the Company’s responsibility to maintain safe and reliable service to its customers.   
 
Capital investment estimates, presented below, are based on vendor quotes for EV chargers. They 
are dependent on anticipated load requirements for the total proposed number of EVs at each 
Company site being met.    
Rationale:  
 
The EV Charger effort is part of a broader investment proposed by the Company to convert fleet 
ICE vehicles to EVs as the former come due for replacement. The broader investment calls for 
conversions beginning in FY25 and running through FY28. To meet that EV conversion timeline, 
which proposes EV adoption in line with the existing fleet replacement schedules, EV chargers 
and associated make-ready work must be completed at locations where ICE fleet vehicles are 
nearing end of life in advance of the replacement schedule. Without sufficient charging capability, 
fleet EVs will be unable to meet operational need and may impact the Company’s ability to provide 
safe and reliable service.     
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
1 For a breakdown of the operational expense associated with the proposed Fleet EV Program, see Exhibit ___ 
(GIOP-11)

Customer Benefit:    
 
As proposed, the conversion of National Grid’s fleet to EVs will not negatively impact customer 
service or reliability. It mitigates the impact of harmful GHG emissions, will improve local air 
quality, reduce noise pollution, and demonstrate the capability of an electrified fleet to similar end-
users. 
Alternatives:  
 
1. Do Nothing – Not investing in EV charger and make-ready work impacts fleet EV 
adoption, rendering the latter challenging to implement, if not impossible. As a result, the 
Company will be less able to support GHG emissions reduction targets under the CLCPA.  
 
2. Defer the Project – As electrification efforts continue to increase in the communities that 
National Grid serves, increased load demand has the potential to trigger more costly make-
ready upgrades that will correspondingly increase the Company’s (and customer’s) share 
of those costs. Being one of the “first in line” has the benefit of avoiding these potential 
cost increases and delivery delays. Deferring will also push the EV adoption timeline out 
of sync with the current replacement schedule, resulting in an added 5-7 years before the 
Company is able to begin electrifying its fleet in a meaningful way.

Business Contact: Therese Sullivan 
Initiative Title: Mobile Charging Solution 
 
Brief Description: 
 
Mobile charging solutions are needed to support the Company’s initiative to electrify its fleet 
without impacting operations or emergency response capabilities. Among other benefits, mobile 
charging solutions that can provide site backup generation are needed to keep fleet electric vehicles 
(EVs) charged and operational during a storm or emergency event involving widespread electric 
service outages. Having mobile charging solutions will enable the Company’s field force to 
respond to customer outages/emergencies in a timely manner, even if electric outages at Company 
facilities are unable to provide charging for the Company’s fleet.  Vendors have presented an array 
of options, including battery storage, hydrogen, diesel, and natural gas charging solutions in a 
range of sizes and with varied charging capabilities.  Options will be prioritized that are both fit 
for purpose and that present the lowest greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions impact. 
 
The Company seeks to purchase mobile charging solutions, instead of leasing or renting them, to 
avoid supply-chain or market disruptions. The proposed investment of $1.500 million in Data Year 
1 and $1.500 million in Data Year 2 is based on vendor estimates for solutions that will be adequate 
to provide charging needs to the portion of the fleet that the Company seeks to electrify by those 
years.    
Rationale: 
 
Electrifying the fleet supports CLCPA GHG emissions reduction targets, as well as supporting 
New York State’s EV adopting targets. This requires adapting to the demands of EVs (e.g., 
charging capabilities) without negatively impacting customers or the Company’s mandate to 
provide safe and reliable service.   
Mobile Charging Solution Budget Breakdown:

RRP-3, Schedule 9, Workpapers 4, 7, 10 and 13 
 
Customer Benefit: 
 
Mobile charging solutions ensure that the Company can continue to electrify its fleet without risk 
of negatively effecting its ability to respond to customer outages during a major storm or 
emergency event.  Additionally, local air quality will improve, and noise pollution will be reduced 
in communities served by an electric fleet.  
 
 
Exhibit____(GIOP-8) 
Page 12 of 13


--- Page 421 ---
Alternatives: 
 
1. Do Nothing – The current replacement schedule for light-duty fleet vehicles is 
approximately 5-7 years. Replacing the existing fleet with new internal combustion (ICE) 
vehicles will result in the continued reliance on fossil fuels that generate GHG emissions. 
This course would be detrimental to the emissions reduction targets set out in the CLCPA 
for a minimum of 5-7 more years.  
 
2. Lease – Because they are relatively new to the market, leasing mobile charging solutions 
subjects the Company to market uncertainty and cost volatility, and because of that, 
presents risk to the Company’s ability to secure mobile charging solutions on schedule, 
and its fitness to respond to storm or other emergency events.  
 
3. Rent – The risk of being unable to procure a short-term rental during a major storm or 
emergency event is significant because of increased demand, and as a result, a rental 
solution is not being prioritized.

Large Scale Hydrogen Storage and Distribution in New York State 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 


--- Page 427 ---
      
         
                        
 
LARGE SCALE HYDROGEN STORAGE AND DISTRIBUTION IN NEW YORK STATE 
A Joint NYSERDA/National Grid Project  
 
April 2023 
 
Background 
Stony Brook University, through its Institute of Gas Innovation and Technology (I-GIT) and in 
partnership with National Grid, submitted the titled proposal in response to the New York State 
Energy Research and Development Authority (NYSERDA) PON 3249. The proposal was selected 
for funding in December 2019, with cost-share provided by National Grid. This was a first-of-its-
kind proposal on hydrogen blending with natural gas with a vision to: 1) repurpose the multi-
billion-dollar natural gas pipeline infrastructure for hydrogen storage and transport, and 2) 
catch up with the European countries where hydrogen blending was progressing at a fast pace 
(Table 1). However, due to pandemic, the project start was delayed by almost a year. However, 
Stony Brook University also started a partnership with the National Renewable Energy 
Laboratory to develop a national project to assess the challenges to hydrogen blending. The 
concept resulted in a $14 million/2-year HyBlend CRADA project in 2021, funded by US DOE, 
with over 20 industry partners and four National Laboratories (NREL, SNL, PNNL, ANL) and 
Stony Brook University. This summary only discusses results from the NYSERDA/National Grid 
funded study.      
 
Study Highlights 
Our study complements stress/fatigue studies that are being conducted under the U.S. DOE 
funded HyBlend CRADA project, of which Stony Brook University is a partner. In our study, we 
sought to establish changes at the micro and nano levels and correlate H2 exposure time and 
Exhibit____(GIOP-11) 
Page 1 of 17


--- Page 428 ---
concentration to changes in the chemical composition and other electronic state changes within 
the exposed samples. 
The replacement value of existing national natural gas pipelines infrastructure is estimated 
to be over USD one trillion. This study primarily focused on establishing whether this asset can 
be repurposed to ultimately store and transport hydrogen, though transitioning with hydrogen 
blends. We designed and built a test unit to mimic the pipeline and developed procedures to 
study pipe material interaction with hydrogen. The studies were targeted to understand the 
material- hydrogen interaction by using a suite of characterization techniques to detect changes 
at micro and nano scales. The techniques used were Scanning Electron Microscopy (SEM)-EDX 
(Energy Dispersive X-ray), X-ray diffraction (XRD), and X-ray Photoelectron Spectroscopy (XPS), 
mostly at the Center for Functional Nanomaterials at Brookhaven National Laboratory. Below we 
highlight key observations so far.   
 
Safety Studies. The codes and standards for large-scale hydrogen production, storage, and 
transport are being worked out by respective federal agencies, Department of Transportation, 
EPA and others. We are closely monitoring the activities of the Center for Hydrogen Safety, an 
entity 
set 
up 
under 
AIChE, 
to 
help 
with 
hydrogen 
safety 
related 
issues 
(https://www.aiche.org/chs). Below we summarize relevant information on Hydrogen safety that 
we have collected so far.   
 Safety Review Summary. 1) Many existing codes touch on the use of hydrogen blends in 
pipelines and stationary applications (e.g. building heating, engine generators), 2) 
Historically, “manufactured gas” had a high percentage of hydrogen and some aspects of 
blend use can be considered already covered, 3) Standards for construction requirements, 
test fuels, and performance requirements for the listing approval of components are not 
in place and need to be developed, 4) Hydrogen blends need to be addressed in next 
generations of international and national fuel gas codes, 5) ASME codes for pipeline use 
and storage of blends, to 100% hydrogen need to be updated, 6) Updated codes and 
standards need to be incorporated (largely by reference) in future international, state, and 
local building codes. A partial list of relevant to handling large-scale hydrogen are: 1) ASME 
Exhibit____(GIOP-11) 
Page 2 of 17


--- Page 429 ---
B31.8, 2) ASME B31.8s, 3) ASME B31.12, 4) NFPA 2, 5) NFPA 34, 6) NFPA 54 – National Fuel 
Gas Code, 7) NFPA 55, 8) NFPA 67, 68, 69, 9) NFPA 497, 10) UL/ANSI/CSA component listing 
requirement codes (e.g. for burners), 11) ISO 14687, 12) International Building Codes, 13) 
State and Local Building Codes. We are continuing to monitor and update this list as more 
codes and standards are Codes are developed.   
 
Studies on Effect of Hydrogen on Metal and Plastics Pipeline Materials. We are studying the 
effect of hydrogen on both metal and plastic materials. The approach is to image samples of a 
pipe sample, subject it to a desired hydrogen pressure and length of time, then compare the 
before and after    images to discern any changes at the micro or nano levels, depending on the 
imaging technique. The imaging results, specifically using X-ray Photoelectron Spectroscopy (XPS) 
for metals and Atomic Force Microscopy (AFM) for plastics, are discussed below.   
 
 Pipeline Sample Preparation Procedures. We received pipeline samples from National 
Grid. These pipeline cuts originated from sections of pipelines that were taken out of 
service. We devised a procedure to cut 1-inch x 1-inch (coupons) samples (see Figures 1 
and 2). The coupon preparation was necessary for sample to fit in the holders for 
spectroscopic analysis. These samples could be soaked with hydrogen in the customized 
unit at various pressures and then spectroscopically evaluated (see below).  
 
 Customized Sample Test Unit Design and Construction. A customized unit was designed 
and constructed (Figure 3) at Stony Brook University. The unit was fitted with 
thermocouples for temperature (20o – 100o C) and transducers for pressure monitoring 
and digital readouts to ± oC and ± 1 psig accuracy. The system could be operated at up to 
1500 psig. Samples were then exposed to Hydrogen for various time periods (from week 
to months) in the unit. A glove box, filled with Argon gas, was used to store hydrogen-
exposed samples for long periods of time to avoid exposure to air, thus without 
compromising the sample integrity.      
      
Exhibit____(GIOP-11) 
Page 3 of 17


--- Page 430 ---
 Establish Elemental Composition of Samples. The elemental composition of pipeline 
samples- as received- was unknown. We devised a method to first establish their 
elemental composition using Inductively Coupled Plasma Mass Spectrometry (ICP-MS) 
method. Samples were prepared and analyzed. This was crucial to establish any changes 
in the chemical composition after hydrogen exposure. The Fe content of each of three 
samples is summarized in Table 2. The second column is the standard deviation of 
measurements.    
 
 SEM-EDX Data on Metal Pipes. The SEM images provide information about the surfaces 
of a metal. Figures 4 is the scanned surface of samples 3 but similar images were observed 
for Samples 8 and 15. The surfaces of all three samples are uneven. The EDX spectrum can 
provide information on the elemental composition of the sample and the quantitative 
weight percent can be calculated from the peak heights. A sample EDX spectrum of 
Sample 3 is shown in Figure 3. The elemental data for samples 3 and 8 is summarized in 
Table 3. For reference, a fresh sample of stainless steel is also included. Note that the Fe 
content of the sample is similar: 73.76%, 71.22% and 72.38%, respectively for samples 3, 
8 and reference.  
 
 Metal Pipes. Chemical composition Changes in Metal Pipe Surfaces. The XPS instrument 
set up at the Center for Functional Nanomaterials (CFN) at Brookhaven National 
Laboratory is shown in Figure 6. Three used metal pipeline samples (see Figures 1 and 2) 
were characterized. The samples varied in age from 55 to 75 years underground delivering 
gas at 60 psig to New Yorkers.  
o As shown in Table 2, the pipeline samples tested largely contained Fe. Since Fe can 
exist in multiple oxidation states, the XPS technique can track changes in the 
oxidation state of Fe, before and after hydrogen exposure. Figure 7 is an overlay of 
data before and after hydrogen exposure. A comparison of the XPS imaging data 
from three metal pipe samples shows that in all cases, the main component on the 
pipe surface is Iron in the Fe2+ and Fe3+ states, and no reduction to metallic Fe was 
Exhibit____(GIOP-11) 
Page 4 of 17


--- Page 431 ---
observed after extended exposure (up to one month) to 100 psi of pure H2: the 
conclusion was reached by tracking changes in the Fe 3p core level spectra (Figure 
7). If hydrogen was reacting with the pipeline materials, metallic Fe would have 
been observed. This result is noteworthy and suggests that the integrity of the pipe 
is not compromised over one month under high pressure of pure hydrogen.  
        
o We also followed changes in the C and O elements in the pipeline samples. Small 
changes were observed in the C 1s and O 1s core level spectra, which are 
associated with the removal of oxygenated carbonaceous species upon hydrogen 
exposure (Figures 8 and 9). These measurements were carried out under 
conditions to protect the samples from exposure to air (thus no oxidation) 
between the high-pressure hydrogen treatment and the acquisition of XPS 
spectrum, so the observed changes are beyond the error range. Note that the 
analyzed pipe samples were over several decades old, so are expected to be 
somewhat oxidized during operation in the field. We are continuing to conduct 
characterization over longer term (after 2-3 months exposure to hydrogen) to 
firmly establish whether the C and O content varies further or stops after initial 
removal as was observed in Figures 8 and 9.   
 
 Plastic Pipes. Monitoring Surface Changes with Hydrogen exposure. We conducted 
AFM imaging for three samples: 1) Low Density Polyethylene (LDPE), 2) High Density 
Polyethylene (HDPE), 3) Polypropylene (iPP): Samples were prepared according to 
established procedures. Then, the samples were exposed to 150psig H2 for 27 hours and 
the AFM images were recorded before and after exposure to H2. A comparison of the 
before and after images showed essentially no significant changes in the surface 
crystalline morphologies with hydrogen exposure for HDPE and iPP, whereas for LDPE, 
some changes were evident from the images (Figure 10).  Since LDPE are not used 
commercially, we will not test these in further studies.  We will focus on HDPE and iPP 
samples.    
Exhibit____(GIOP-11) 
Page 5 of 17


--- Page 432 ---
 
 Pipeline Modeling Study. The study focused on establishing the energy penalty when 
mixing hydrogen in various ratios with natural gas. The modeling used ANSYS software for 
Computational Fluid Dynamics (CFD) simulated study under turbulent flow conditions 
(High Reynolds number) showed: 1) Higher hydrogen concentrations in the hydrogen-
methane blends require more energy for transportation, 2) Pipelines that have larger 
surface roughness require greater energy for transporting gas blends, 3) Pipelines with 
larger diameters are relatively more energy efficient, 4) Pipeline bend sections introduce 
an additional energy penalty, 5) The methane-hydrogen gas blends tend to develop a core-
annular flow pattern under steady state conditions (Figure 11 and 12). 
 
 Modeling and Experimental Results: A recent experimental study, published in the 
journal “Hydrogen” unequivocally demonstrated the absence of gas stratification in the 
H2/methane gas mixtures in all ratios (between 0% to 50% H2 (v/v)). So far, we only 
modeled the behavior of the H2 blended methane mixture under high Reynolds number 
(turbulent flow) that shows H2 separation from methane under conditions that mimic gas 
moving in a pipeline. The experimental study was conducted to mimic gas distribution 
lines that normally operate under laminar flow.          
 
 Refereed Publication. A recent experimental study from National Grid, published in a 
refereed journal “Hydrogen”, directly demonstrates that hydrogen blended natural gas 
mixtures remain homogenous in distribution piping regardless of the blend ratio or 
operating pressure in the range tested, (0–50% (v/v)). 
 
Ongoing Work 
The data presented in this report shows promise for repurposing the existing natural gas pipeline 
infrastructure to carry hydrogen. Our strategy is to image Fe and follow any hydrogen induced 
changes under high hydrogen pressures (300-500 psig). Though our initial pipeline integrity data 
looks promising, longer term (4-6 months) data is desirable. Therefore, the characterization of 
Exhibit____(GIOP-11) 
Page 6 of 17


--- Page 433 ---
metal and plastic pipe samples continues. A detailed report that will include more findings will 
be released by the end of summer 2023.
